Out of the two time-reflected solutions of the Euler equation, only the one with the negative normal strain survives. If virtually created as a metastable phase, the other dissolves in the turbulent flow, but this remains stable.

Technically this instability displays itself in the lack of the real solutions of the steady Navier-Stokes equation for positive
\hat{S}_{nn}
. The Gaussian profile of vorticity as a function of normal coordinate formally becomes complex at positive
\hat{S}_{nn}
, which means instability or decay in the time-dependent equation.

In
[
23
]
, the authors verified this decay/instability process. They solved the time-dependent Navier-Stokes equation numerically in the vicinity of the steady solution with arbitrary background strain.
Only the Burgers-Townsend solution corresponding to our
CVS
conditions on the strain was stable.

No external forces drive the breaking of the time-reversal symmetry of the Euler dynamics; it rather emerges spontaneously by the stability requirement of internal Navier-Stokes dynamics. The result of this microscopic stability mechanism of the Navier-Stokes dynamics is the
CVS
boundary conditions added to the ambiguous Euler dynamics of the vortex sheets.

4.12
Complex Curves

Let us proceed with the exact solution of the
CVS
equations.

The
CVS
equations for the cylindrical geometry reduce to the equation (
4.73
) for the boundary loop
C(\theta)
plus a complex equation
V_{+}(x,y)+V_{-}(x,y)=0
at the loop
(x,y)\in C
.

Consider two holomorphic functions involved in this equation:
F_{-}(\eta)
inside the loop,
F_{+}(\eta)
outside.
The boundary condition relates these two functions

\displaystyle F_{+}(\eta)+F_{-}(\eta)+(a-b)\eta-c\bar{\eta}=0;\forall\eta\in C;

(4.77)

Let us look for the linear solution of the Laplace equation inside (constant strain):

\displaystyle F_{-}(\eta)=(p+\imath\,q)\eta

(4.78)

with some real
p,q
.

The vanishing normal velocity from the inside leads to the differential equation for
C(\theta)
.

\displaystyle\textbf{Im }\left((2p+a-b+2\imath\,q)C(\theta)-c\bar{C}(\theta)\right)C^{\prime}(\theta)=0

(4.79)

This equation is integrable for arbitrary parameters.
We shall use two dimensionless parameters
\gamma,\beta
to parametrize
p,q
as follows:

\displaystyle p+\imath\,q=\frac{b-a}{2}+\frac{ce^{-2\imath\,\beta}}{2(2\gamma+1)};

(4.80)

The general solution of (
4.79
) in polar coordinates reads
[
44
]
(up to an arbitrary normalization constant)

\displaystyle C(\theta)=e^{\imath\,\beta}(1+\imath\,\tau)\tau^{\gamma};

(4.81)

\displaystyle\tau=\tan\theta

(4.82)

This solution applies when
\tau^{\gamma}
is real.

4.13
Parabolic curves

Let us consider positive
\gamma
first (the parabolic curves).

This solution passes through the origin and will have no singularity at
\tau=0
in case
\gamma=n,n\in\mathbb{Z},n\geq 0
.

The case
n=0
corresponds to a tilted straight line.

Figure 5:
The Burgers-Townsend case,
n=0
.

This case is just a Burgers-Townsend planar vortex sheet.
The next cases are already nontrivial.

Figure 6:
The
CVS
for
n=1
.

Figure 7:
The
CVS
for
n=2
.

Figure 8:
The
CVS
for
n=3
.

However, not all positive
n
are acceptable. For even positive
n
we have a cusp in a curve because only positive
r(\theta)
exist at
\theta\rightarrow 0
. This cusp is visible at the
n=2
curve.

For odd positive
n=2k+1
, there is a linear relation between
x,y
at small
\tau
provided
\beta\neq 0
.
The slope of the curve
y(x)
at
x=\pm 0
is the same.

Higher derivatives are singular, as we have

\displaystyle x\sim\tau^{2k+1}\cos\left(\beta\right)(1-\tan\left(\beta\right)\tau);

(4.83)

\displaystyle y\sim\tau^{2k+1}\sin\left(\beta\right)(1+\cot\left(\beta\right)\tau);

(4.84)

\displaystyle y\rightarrow\tan\left(\beta\right)x\left(1+\frac{2\sign x}{\sin\beta}\left(\frac{|x|}{\cos\left(\beta\right)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2k+1}}\right);

(4.85)

\displaystyle\frac{dy}{dx}\rightarrow\tan\left(\beta\right)\text{ at }x\rightarrow\pm 0

(4.86)

As the slopes
\frac{dy}{dx}
are the same at
x=\pm 0
, the vortex sheet reduces to a plane up to higher-order terms. Thus, the Burgers-Townsend solution, assuming the infinitesimal thickness of the vorticity layer, will still apply here. Ergo, the sheet will be stable at this point, and the points on the rest of the surface.

Thus, we have found the discrete parametric family of
CVS
shapes. It simplifies in terms of
\tau=\tan\theta
.

\displaystyle\eta(\tau)=e^{\imath\,\beta}\left(1+\imath\,\tau\right)\tau^{2k+1};

(4.87)

\displaystyle k\in\mathbb{Z},k\geq 0;

(4.88)

\displaystyle-\infty<\tau<\infty;

(4.89)

We have a conformal map from the
\tau
plane to the plane of
\eta=x+\imath\,y
, with our curve
C
corresponding to the real axis. The physical region outside the loop corresponds to the sector in the lower semiplane,
-\frac{\pi}{(k+1)}<\arg\tau<0
.

The infinity in the physical space corresponds to infinity in the
\tau
plane.

Two singular points correspond to the vanishing derivative
\eta^{\prime}(\tau)=0
. The singularity happens at

\displaystyle\tau=0;

(4.90)

\displaystyle\tau=\frac{\imath\,(2k+1)}{2(k+1)};

(4.91)

The first one maps to the origin in the
\eta
plane. The second one is in the upper
\tau
semiplane, so it does not affect the inverse function
\tau(\eta)
in the physical sector of the lower semiplane.

The next step is to find the holomorphic function
F_{+}(\eta)
, which defines the complex velocity on the external side of the sheet (the internal side has linear velocity
F_{-}(\eta)=(p+\imath\,q)\eta
).

From the
CVS
equation, we get the boundary values, which we express in terms of
\eta,\eta^{*}

\displaystyle F_{+}(\eta)=-(p+a-b+\imath\,q)\eta+c\eta^{*};\;\forall\eta\in C

(4.92)

We have to continue this function from the curve (
4.87
) into the part of the complex plane outside this curve.

Let us express the right side in terms of
\tau

\displaystyle f(\tau)=-(p+a-b+\imath\,q)\eta(\tau)+c\tilde{\eta}(\tau)

(4.93)

Here
\tilde{\eta}(\tau)
is the complex conjugate expression for
\eta

\displaystyle\tilde{\eta}(\tau)=e^{-\imath\,\beta}\left(1-\imath\,\tau\right)\tau^{2k+1};

(4.94)

At the real axis of
\tau
the variables
\eta(\tau),\tilde{\eta}(\tau)
will be complex conjugates, and the boundary value

\displaystyle f(\tau)=F_{+}(\eta(\tau));\forall\textbf{Im }\tau=0

(4.96)

Now we have an obvious analytic continuation to the lower semiplane of
\tau
. After some algebra, we get
[
44
]

\displaystyle\eta(\tau)=e^{\imath\,\beta}\left(1+\imath\,\tau\right)\tau^{2k+1};

(4.97)

\displaystyle f(\tau)=\frac{1}{2}e^{-i\beta}\tau^{2k+1}

\displaystyle\left(\frac{c(-i(8k+7)\tau+8k+5)}{4k+3}-ie^{2i\beta}(\tau-i)(2a+c)\right)

(4.98)

This parametric function in the sector
-\frac{\pi}{(k+1)}<\arg\tau<0
of the complex
\tau
plane represents an implicit solution of the
CVS
equation.

Now, let us consider the limit
\tau\rightarrow\infty
corresponding to
\eta\rightarrow\infty
. In this limit, the function
F_{+}(\eta(\tau))
linearly grows

\displaystyle F_{+}(\eta)\rightarrow B\eta;

(4.99)

\displaystyle B=-a+\frac{1}{2}c\left(-1-\frac{e^{-2i\beta}(8k+7)}{4k+3}\right)

(4.100)

This linear growth means that the true value of the
xy
components of strain at infinity is not
\hbox{diag}\left(a,-c-a\right)
we thought it was. It is instead

\displaystyle\hat{S}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\frac{c((8k+7)\cos(2\beta)+4k+3)}{8k+6}&-\frac{c(8k+7)\sin(2\beta)}{8k+6}\\
-\frac{c(8k+7)\sin(2\beta)}{8k+6}&\frac{c((8k+7)\cos(2\beta)-4k-3)}{8k+6}\\
\end{array}\right)

The eigenvalues of this strain are

\displaystyle\left\{-\frac{c(6k+5)}{4k+3},\frac{2c(k+1)}{4k+3}\right\}

(4.104)

The angle
\beta
dropped from the eigenvalues because it can be eliminated by the
U(1)
transformation
\eta\rightarrow\eta e^{-\imath\,\beta}
, which leaves the eigenvalues invariant.

The parabolic solution does not decrease at infinity, which makes it unphysical.

4.14
Hyperbolic curves

Let us now consider the hyperbolic curves with negative
\gamma<0
, which do not need to be half-integer.
We redefine our parameters as follows:

\displaystyle\eta(\xi)=\pm e^{\imath\,\beta}\left(\xi+\imath\,\xi^{-\mu}\right);

(4.105)

\displaystyle\xi=\tau^{\gamma};

(4.106)

\displaystyle\mu=-1-\frac{1}{\gamma};\mu>0;

(4.107)

\displaystyle 0<\xi<\infty

(4.108)

These two curves correspond to various
\mu,\beta
.

Figure 9:
The hyperbolic
CVS
for
\beta=\pi/3,\mu=2.5
.

Figure 10:
The hyperbolic
CVS
for
\beta=0,\mu=2.5

Note that there are no singularities at the origin, as both curves are going around it.

The inverse function is singular at the point
\xi_{0}
in the upper semiplane
where
\eta^{\prime}(\xi_{0})=0
:

\displaystyle\xi_{0}=(i\mu)^{\frac{1}{\mu+1}}

(4.109)

Therefore, we need to use the lower semiplane as a physical domain for
f(\xi)=F_{+}(\eta(\xi))
.

The holomorphic function
f(\xi)
is reconstructed employing the same steps as in the parabolic case. We get

\displaystyle f(\xi)=\frac{1}{2}e^{-i\beta}\xi\left(\frac{c(\mu-3)}{\mu-1}-e^{2i\beta}(2a+c)\right)-

\displaystyle\frac{ie^{-i\beta}\xi^{-\mu}\left(c(3\mu-1)+e^{2i\beta}(\mu-1)(2a+c)\right)}{2(\mu-1)}

(4.110)

Let us now find the limit at
\xi\rightarrow\infty
, when
\eta
and
f
both linearly grow

\displaystyle f\rightarrow\eta B;

(4.111)

\displaystyle B=-a+\frac{1}{2}c\left(-1+\frac{e^{-2i\beta}(\mu-3)}{\mu-1}\right)

(4.112)

We want this coefficient to be zero so that
f(\eta)
decreases at infinity.

The solution of this complex equation for
\beta,\mu
is

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\beta=0;&\mu=1-\frac{c}{a};\\
\beta=\frac{1}{2}\pi;&\mu=1-\frac{c}{b};\\
\end{cases}

(4.113)

From the inequalities between the three eigenvalues
a<b<c
adding to zero, we get

\displaystyle-2c<a<-\frac{c}{2};

(4.114)

\displaystyle-\frac{c}{2}<b<c;

(4.115)

which makes this index
\mu
limited to the intervals

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\beta=0;&\frac{3}{2}<\mu<3;\\
\beta=\frac{1}{2}\pi;&-\infty<\mu<\infty;\end{cases}

(4.116)

We choose the first solution, as in this case,
\mu
varies in the positive region where there are no singularities.

Now, we observe that at
\beta=0,\frac{1}{2}\pi
, which means
q=0
there is an extra symmetry in the equation (
4.79
)

\displaystyle C\Rightarrow C^{*}

(4.117)

This symmetry means that now all four branches of the hyperbola

\displaystyle|y||x|^{\mu}=\mbox{const};

(4.118)

are the parts of a single periodic (unbounded) solution
C(\theta)
(Fig
18
).

These four branches define the domain inside the loop
C
where the holomorphic velocity

\displaystyle F_{-}(\eta)=-2a\eta;

(4.119)

As for the function
F_{+}
it is just a negative power

\displaystyle F_{+}\left(x\pm\imath\,|x|^{-\mu}\right)=+2\imath\,c|x|^{-\mu};

(4.120)

The loop
C(\theta)
defined by these four branches is a periodic function with singularities at
\theta=k\pi/2
.

Topologically, on a Riemann sphere
S_{2}
, these four branches divide the sphere into five regions.
There is an inside region, with the boundary touching Riemann infinity four times.

Each of the four remaining outside regions is bounded by a hyperbola, starting and ending at infinity.

Figure 11:
The stereographic projection of four hyperbola branches on the Riemann sphere.

Let us consider the lower right hyperbola with
y=-x^{-\mu},x>0

The values of
F_{+}(\eta)
in three other external regions will be obtained from this one by reflection against the
x
or
y
axis.

\displaystyle F_{+}(-\eta)=-F_{+}(\eta);

(4.121)

\displaystyle F_{+}(\eta^{*})=F_{+}^{*}(\eta);

(4.122)

We can continue the above parametric equation for the function
f
to the complex plane for the variable
w
, with the cut from
-\infty
to
0
. The phase of the multivalued function
w^{-\mu}
is set to
\arg w^{-\mu}=0,w>0
.

\displaystyle\eta=w-\imath\,w^{-\mu};

(4.123)

\displaystyle F_{+}=2\imath\,cw^{-\mu};

(4.124)

This parametric function has a square root singularity at the point where derivative
\partial_{w}\eta=1+\imath\,\mu w^{-\mu-1}
vanishes:

\displaystyle w_{c}=(-\imath\,\mu)^{\frac{1}{\mu+1}}

(4.125)

For positive
\mu
, this singularity is located in the lower semiplane of
w
, leaving the upper semiplane as a physical domain.

We have drawn the complex maps

7

Mathematica

®

is a 3D surface of
x,y,|f(x+\imath\,y)|
for complex function
f(z)
with its phase

as color of the point at this surface.
of the derivative
\partial_{w}\eta=1+\imath\,\mu w^{-\mu-1}
for

.

. The square root singularities of the inverse function
\eta(w)

. We indicate them as holes on the surface (white circles). The black lines are described by

. The physical region is outside the black line in the first quadrant, and there are no singularities.

. We indicate them as holes on the surface (white circles). The black lines are described by

. The physical region is outside the black line in the first quadrant, and there are no singularities.

for

. The height is

\eta(w)
