By rescaling the matrix elements
W_{ij}=\sigma w_{ij}
we find the property

\displaystyle Z_{n}(\sigma)=\sigma^{\frac{(n+2)(n-1)}{2}}Z_{n}(1)

(B.5)

Taking the logarithmic derivative of the original integral, we get the identity

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma Z_{n}^{\prime}(\sigma)}{Z_{n}(\sigma)}=\frac{\intop\nolimits dP_{\sigma}(W)\hbox{tr}\,W^{2}}{\sigma^{2}Z_{n}(\sigma)}=\frac{\left\langle\hbox{tr}\,W^{2}\right\rangle}{\sigma^{2}}

(B.6)

On the other hand, taking the logarithmic derivative from (
B.5
), we find

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma Z_{n}^{\prime}(\sigma)}{Z_{n}(\sigma)}=\frac{(n+2)(n-1)}{2}

(B.7)

This formula produces the trace relation (
4.173
).

Appendix C
Spherical Gauge

The symmetric metric tensor
g_{ij}
in 2 dimensions has three independent components: two diagonal values
g_{11},g_{22}
and one off-diagonal value
g_{12}=g_{21}
.

We take stereographic coordinates
z=z_{1}+\imath\,z_{2}=\tan\frac{\theta}{2}e^{\imath\,\varphi}

\displaystyle g_{ij}=\delta_{ij}\rho;

(C.1a)

\displaystyle\rho=\frac{1}{\left(1+|z|^{2}\right)^{2}};

(C.1b)

\displaystyle z_{a}=\frac{S_{a}}{1+S_{3}};

(C.1c)

\displaystyle S_{a}=\frac{2z_{a}}{1+|z|^{2}};

(C.1d)

\displaystyle S_{3}=\frac{1-|z|^{2}}{1+|z|^{2}};

(C.1e)

\displaystyle d^{2}S=dz_{1}dz_{2}\rho

(C.1f)

The
O(3)
rotation in these coordinates reads (with
I,J,K=1,2,3,a,b,c...=1,2
)

\displaystyle\delta S_{I}=e_{IJK}S_{J}\alpha_{k};

(C.2a)

\displaystyle\delta z_{a}=\alpha_{3}e_{ab}z_{b}-\frac{1}{2}(1-|z|^{2})\tilde{\alpha}_{a}-\tilde{\alpha}_{b}z_{b}z_{a};

(C.2b)

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}_{b}=e_{bc}\alpha_{c};

(C.2c)

The
O(3)
transformation of the metric tensor involves the matrix
R_{ij}=\partial_{j}\delta z_{i}

\displaystyle R_{ij}=\alpha_{3}e_{ij}+\tilde{\alpha}_{i}z_{j}-\tilde{\alpha}_{j}z_{i}-\delta_{ij}\tilde{\alpha}z;

(C.3)

\displaystyle\delta_{O(3)}g_{ij}=R_{ai}g_{aj}+R_{aj}g_{ai}

(C.4)

Computing the variation
\delta_{O(3)}g_{ij}
of the conformal metric
g^{c}_{ij}=\rho\delta_{ij}
we find

\displaystyle\delta_{O(3)}g^{c}_{12}=0;

(C.5a)

\displaystyle\delta_{O(3)}g^{c}_{11}=-2\rho\left(z_{1}\alpha_{2}-z_{2}\alpha_{1}\right);

(C.5b)

\displaystyle\delta_{O(3)}g^{c}_{22}=-2\rho\left(z_{1}\alpha_{2}-z_{2}\alpha_{1}\right);

(C.5c)

The gauge transformation of conformal metrics produces

\displaystyle\delta_{gauge}g^{c}_{12}=(h_{22}-h_{11})\rho;

(C.6a)

\displaystyle\delta_{gauge}g^{c}_{11}=2h_{12}\rho;

(C.6b)

\displaystyle\delta_{gauge}g^{c}_{22}=-2h_{12}\rho;

(C.6c)

\displaystyle h_{ij}=\partial_{i}\partial_{j}h(z_{1},z_{2})

(C.6d)

Now, we do not want to break the rotational invariance of the spherical metric.
Therefore, any
h(z)
satisfying the equations

\displaystyle h_{22}-h_{11}=0;

(C.7)

\displaystyle h_{12}=-(z_{1}\alpha_{2}-z_{2}\alpha_{1});

(C.8)

\displaystyle-h_{12}=-(z_{1}\alpha_{2}-z_{2}\alpha_{1})

(C.9)

with some finite constant
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
should not be restricted by our gauge conditions. Adding the last two equations, we immediately see that no such gauge functions could imitate the
O(3)
rotations.

The independent conditions
h_{12}=0,h_{11}=h_{22}
combine into one complex equation

\hat{L}h=\rho\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\bar{z}^{2}}h=0

(C.10)

These gauge conditions leave out arbitrary linear function
h=A+B_{i}z_{i}
, corresponding to constant shifts of Clebsch field. These constant shifts can be fixed by placing the origin at the South Pole, which we did.

For remaining nontrivial symplectomorphisms we have the gauge fixing Gaussian integral

\displaystyle\intop\nolimits D\lambda D\mu Dh\exp\left(\imath\,\intop\nolimits d^{2}S\lambda\hat{L}_{1}h+\mu\hat{L}_{2}h\right);

(C.11a)

\displaystyle\hat{L}_{1}h=\rho\textbf{Re }\hat{L}h;

(C.11b)

\displaystyle\hat{L}_{2}h=\rho\textbf{Im }\hat{L}h;

(C.11c)

The regularized determinant
\det{\hat{L}}
is a universal number, which does not depend on our dynamical variables.

This operator being non-Hermitian, we are not sure how to regularize and compute this determinant, but this is immaterial, as it does not depend on dynamic variables and thus drops from the measure.

The symmetric metric tensor
g_{ij}
in 2 dimensions has three independent components: two diagonal values
g_{11},g_{22}
and one off-diagonal value
g_{12}=g_{21}
.

We take stereographic coordinates
z=z_{1}+\imath\,z_{2}=\tan\frac{\theta}{2}e^{\imath\,\varphi}

\displaystyle g_{ij}=\delta_{ij}\rho;

(C.12a)

\displaystyle\rho=\frac{1}{\left(1+|z|^{2}\right)^{2}};

(C.12b)

\displaystyle z_{a}=\frac{S_{a}}{1+S_{3}};

(C.12c)

\displaystyle S_{a}=\frac{2z_{a}}{1+|z|^{2}};

(C.12d)

\displaystyle S_{3}=\frac{1-|z|^{2}}{1+|z|^{2}};

(C.12e)

\displaystyle d^{2}S=dz_{1}dz_{2}\rho

(C.12f)

The
O(3)
rotation in these coordinates reads (with
I,J,K=1,2,3,a,b,c...=1,2
)

\displaystyle\delta S_{I}=e_{IJK}S_{J}\alpha_{k};

(C.13a)

\displaystyle\delta z_{a}=\alpha_{3}e_{ab}z_{b}-\frac{1}{2}(1-|z|^{2})\tilde{\alpha}_{a}-\tilde{\alpha}_{b}z_{b}z_{a};

(C.13b)

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}_{b}=e_{bc}\alpha_{c};

(C.13c)

The
O(3)
transformation of the metric tensor involves the matrix
R_{ij}=\partial_{j}\delta z_{i}

\displaystyle R_{ij}=\alpha_{3}e_{ij}+\tilde{\alpha}_{i}z_{j}-\tilde{\alpha}_{j}z_{i}-\delta_{ij}\tilde{\alpha}z;

(C.14)

\displaystyle\delta_{O(3)}g_{ij}=R_{ai}g_{aj}+R_{aj}g_{ai}

(C.15)

Computing the variation
\delta_{O(3)}g_{ij}
of the conformal metric
g^{c}_{ij}=\rho\delta_{ij}
we find

\displaystyle\delta_{O(3)}g^{c}_{12}=0;

(C.16a)

\displaystyle\delta_{O(3)}g^{c}_{11}=-2\rho\left(z_{1}\alpha_{2}-z_{2}\alpha_{1}\right);

(C.16b)

\displaystyle\delta_{O(3)}g^{c}_{22}=-2\rho\left(z_{1}\alpha_{2}-z_{2}\alpha_{1}\right);

(C.16c)

The gauge transformation of conformal metrics produces

\displaystyle\delta_{gauge}g^{c}_{12}=(h_{22}-h_{11})\rho;

(C.17a)

\displaystyle\delta_{gauge}g^{c}_{11}=2h_{12}\rho;

(C.17b)

\displaystyle\delta_{gauge}g^{c}_{22}=-2h_{12}\rho;

(C.17c)

\displaystyle h_{ij}=\partial_{i}\partial_{j}h(z_{1},z_{2})

(C.17d)

Now, we do not want to break the rotational invariance of the spherical metric.
Therefore, any
h(z)
satisfying the equations

\displaystyle h_{22}-h_{11}=0;

(C.18)

\displaystyle h_{12}=-(z_{1}\alpha_{2}-z_{2}\alpha_{1});

(C.19)

\displaystyle-h_{12}=-(z_{1}\alpha_{2}-z_{2}\alpha_{1})

(C.20)

with some finite constant
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
should not be restricted by our gauge conditions. Adding the last two equations, we immediately see that no such gauge functions could imitate the
O(3)
rotations.

The independent conditions
h_{12}=0,h_{11}=h_{22}
combine into one complex equation

\hat{L}h=\rho\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\bar{z}^{2}}h=0

(C.21)

These gauge conditions leave out arbitrary linear function
h=A+B_{i}z_{i}
, corresponding to constant shifts of Clebsch field. These constant shifts can be fixed by placing the origin at the South Pole, which we did.

For remaining nontrivial symplectomorphisms we have the gauge fixing Gaussian integral

\displaystyle\intop\nolimits D\lambda D\mu Dh\exp\left(\imath\,\intop\nolimits d^{2}S\lambda\hat{L}_{1}h+\mu\hat{L}_{2}h\right);

(C.22a)

\displaystyle\hat{L}_{1}h=\rho\textbf{Re }\hat{L}h;

(C.22b)

\displaystyle\hat{L}_{2}h=\rho\textbf{Im }\hat{L}h;

(C.22c)

The regularized determinant
\det{\hat{L}}
is a universal number, which does not depend on our dynamical variables.

This operator being non-Hermitian, we are not sure how to regularize and compute this determinant, but this is immaterial, as it does not depend on dynamic variables and thus drops from the measure.

Appendix D
Burgers Vortex contribution to the Hamiltonian of the Kelvinon

Let us compute the contribution of small radial coordinates to the Hamiltonian of the Kelvinon.
We assume the loop to be a straight line in this approximation and take the Burgers vortex solution
[
31
]
for the Navier-Stokes equation in this region.

\displaystyle\vec{v}=\left\{-\alpha x-g(r)y,-\alpha y+g(r)x,2\alpha z\right\};

(D.1)

\displaystyle g(r)=\frac{\left(1-e^{-\frac{\alpha r^{2}}{2\nu}}\right)\Gamma}{2\pi r^{2}};

(D.2)

\displaystyle r=\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}};

(D.3)

\displaystyle\Gamma=4\pi n;

(D.4)

\displaystyle\alpha=\frac{1}{2}\hat{t}\cdot\hat{S}\cdot\hat{t};

(D.5)

\displaystyle\hat{t}=\{0,0,1\}

(D.6)

The contribution to the Euler Hamiltonian from the circular core of the vortex at
z=0
was computed using
Mathematica

®

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\intop\nolimits_{x^{2}+y^{2}<R^{2}}dxdy\,\vec{v}(x,y,0)^{2}=

\displaystyle 2\pi n^{2}\left(\gamma+\log\left(\frac{\alpha R^{2}}{4\nu}\right)\right)+\frac{1}{4}\pi\alpha^{2}R^{4}+

\displaystyle 2\pi n^{2}\text{Ei}\left(-\frac{R^{2}\alpha}{\nu}\right)-4\pi n^{2}\text{Ei}\left(-\frac{R^{2}\alpha}{2\nu}\right)

(D.7)

where
\text{Ei}(x)
is an exponential integral function.

In the turbulent limit
\nu\rightarrow 0
we get

\displaystyle 2\pi n^{2}\left(\gamma+\log\left(\frac{\alpha R^{2}}{4\nu}\right)\right)+\frac{1}{4}\pi\alpha^{2}R^{4}+O\left(\exp\left(-\frac{R^{2}\alpha}{2\nu}\right)\right)

(D.8)

Appendix E
Initial data for the Kelvinon

Let us present an explicit example of the Clebsch field with the required topology, which could serve as initial data for the Hamiltonian minimization by relaxation.

We introduce a surface of the minimal area bounded by our loop
C

\displaystyle S_{min}(C)=\argmin_{S\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\partial S=C}\intop\nolimits_{S}dS

(E.1)

For every point
\vec{r}\in R_{3}
, there is the nearest point
\vec{r}_{1}
at the minimal surface
S_{min}(C)
.

\displaystyle\vec{r}_{1}=\argmin_{\vec{r}^{\prime}\in S_{min}(C)}(\vec{r}-\vec{r}^{\prime})^{2};

(E.2)

For this point
\vec{r}_{1}
at the surface there is also a nearest point
\vec{r}_{0}
at its edge
C
, minimizing the geodesic distance
d(a,b)
along the surface from
\vec{r}_{1}
to the edge

\displaystyle s_{0}=\argmin_{s}d(\vec{r}_{1},\vec{C}(s));

(E.3)

\displaystyle\vec{r}_{0}=\vec{C}(s_{0});

(E.4)

Let us also introduce the local frame with vectors
\vec{t}(s),\vec{n}(s),\vec{\sigma}(s)
at the loop:

\displaystyle\vec{C}^{\prime}(s)^{2}=1;

(E.5a)

\displaystyle\vec{t}(s)=\vec{C}^{\prime}(s);

(E.5b)

\displaystyle\vec{n}(s)=\frac{\vec{C}^{\prime\prime}(s)}{|\vec{C}^{\prime\prime}(s)|}

(E.5c)

\displaystyle\vec{\sigma}(s)=\vec{t}(s)\times\vec{n}(s);

(E.5d)

Our field is then defined as follows:

\displaystyle\alpha=\frac{2\pi s_{0}}{\ointop\nolimits|d\vec{C}|};

(E.6a)

\displaystyle\beta=\arg\left((\vec{r}-\vec{r}_{0})\cdot\left(\vec{n}(s_{0})+\imath\vec{\sigma}(s_{0})\right)\right);

(E.6b)

\displaystyle\rho=\sqrt{(\vec{r}-\vec{r}_{1})^{2}+d(\vec{r}_{1},\vec{r}_{0})^{2}};

(E.6c)

\displaystyle\theta=\frac{\lambda w^{2}+\pi\rho^{2}}{w^{2}+\rho^{2}};

(E.6d)

\displaystyle\phi=m\alpha+n\beta;

(E.6e)

\displaystyle\vec{S}=\left(\sin\theta\cos\phi,\sin\theta\sin\phi,\cos\theta\right);

(E.6f)

The parameter
w
plays the role of the size of the Kelvinon in physical space
R_{3}
.

When the point
\vec{r}
approaches the nearest point
\vec{r}_{1}
at the minimal surface, the difference
\vec{\eta}=\vec{r}-\vec{r}_{1}
is normal to this surface.

When the point
\vec{r}_{1}
approaches the nearest point
\vec{r}_{0}
at the edge
C
of the surface, the geodesic becomes a straight line in
R_{3}
, tangent to the surface and
\rho
becomes Euclidean distance to the loop

\displaystyle d(\vec{r}_{1},\vec{r}_{0})\rightarrow|\vec{r}_{1}-\vec{r}_{0}|;

(E.7)

\displaystyle\rho^{2}\rightarrow|\vec{r}-\vec{r}_{1}|^{2}+|\vec{r}_{1}-\vec{r}_{0}|^{2}=|\vec{r}-\vec{r}_{0}|^{2}

(E.8)

Note that all variables
s_{0},\vec{r}_{0},\vec{r}_{1},\alpha,\beta,\rho,\theta,\phi
depend on
\vec{r}\in R_{3}
through the minimization of the distance to the surface and the loop. By construction,
\rho=|\vec{r}-\vec{r}_{0}|
away from the surface, when
\vec{r}_{1}=\vec{r}_{0},d(\vec{r}_{1},\vec{r}_{0})=0
.

The Euler angles
\theta,\phi
for the Clebsch field take the boundary values at the loop :

\displaystyle\phi(\vec{r}\rightarrow C)\rightarrow m\alpha+n\beta;

(E.9)

\displaystyle\theta(\vec{r}\rightarrow C)=\lambda+O\left((\vec{r}-C)^{2}\right);

(E.10)

and
\theta(\infty)=\pi
.

One may estimate the decay rate of
\vec{\nabla}\cos\theta\sim 1/|\vec{r}|^{3},\vec{\nabla}\phi\sim 1/|\vec{r}|
, which corresponds to vorticity decaying as
1/|\vec{r}|^{4}
.

This decay is sufficient for the convergence of the enstrophy integral at infinity.

Let us move
\vec{r}
along the normal from the surface at
\vec{r}_{1}
. Our parametrization of
\theta
does not change in the first order in normal shift
\vec{\eta}=\vec{r}-\vec{r}_{1}
, as
\rho^{2}
has only quadratic terms in
\vec{\eta}
.

We conclude that the normal derivative of the Clebsch field
\phi_{1}=Z(1+\cos\theta)
vanishes

\displaystyle\partial_{n}\phi_{1}=0;

(E.11)

We requested vanishing normal velocity at the discontinuity surface for this surface to be stationary.

In terms of the Clebsch parametrization, the normal velocity would vanish provided

\displaystyle\partial_{n}\Phi=\left(\vec{\nabla}\times\Psi\right)_{n};\;\vec{r}\in S_{C}\setminus C

(E.12)

As for the angular field
\phi
in (
E.6
), its normal derivative does not vanish in the general case. The angle
\alpha
does not change when the point
\vec{r}
moves in normal direction
\vec{N}(\vec{r}_{1})
from the surface projection
\vec{r}_{1}
by infinitesimal shift
\vec{\eta}=\epsilon\vec{N}(\vec{r}_{1})
. However, another angle
\beta
changes in the linear order in
\epsilon
as

\displaystyle\vec{N}(\vec{r}_{1})\cdot\left(\vec{n}(s_{0})+\imath\vec{\sigma}(s_{0})\right)\neq 0

(E.13)

Appendix F
Asymptotic expansion of the Kelvinon probability tail

The probability distribution (
5.8
) does not reduce to a known special function. However, the terms of asymptotic expansion at large
\Gamma
can be computed analytically.

Integrating out the third component of the vector
\xi
, we find (up to irrelevant constant normalization)

\displaystyle\Pi(\gamma)\propto\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{\mu}{c}\right)}{\sqrt{c\mu}}\intop\nolimits d^{2}\xi\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\xi_{i}^{2}+\frac{\xi_{i}\Lambda_{ij}\xi_{j}}{2c}\right)}{\sqrt{\left(1-\frac{\xi_{i}\Lambda_{ij}\xi_{j}}{2\mu}\right)}};

(F.1)

\displaystyle\mu=\gamma-\tau;

(F.2)

Now, we expand this square root in Taylor expansion in
1/\mu

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\left(1-\frac{\xi_{i}\Lambda_{ij}\xi_{j}}{2\mu}\right)}}=\sum_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\Gamma(n+\frac{1}{2})}{\sqrt{\pi}n!\mu^{n}}\left(\frac{\xi_{i}\Lambda_{ij}\xi_{j}}{2}\right)^{n}

(F.3)

Interchanging the summation and integration and representing

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\xi_{i}\Lambda_{ij}\xi_{j}}{2}\right)^{n}\exp\left(\frac{\xi_{i}\Lambda_{ij}\xi_{j}}{2c}\right)=

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{z}\right)^{n}\left.\exp\left(z\frac{\xi_{i}\Lambda_{ij}\xi_{j}}{2}\right)\right|_{z=1/c}

(F.4)

we use
Mathematica

®

, and get the following asymptotic expansion

\displaystyle\Pi(\gamma)\sim\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{\gamma-\tau}{c}\right)\sqrt{c}}{\sqrt{(c-a)(c-b)(\gamma-\tau)}}\sum_{0}^{\infty}F_{n}\frac{c^{n}}{(\gamma-\tau)^{n}};

(F.5)

\displaystyle F_{n}=\frac{\Gamma\left(n+\frac{1}{2}\right)\left(\frac{ab}{(c-a)(c-b)}\right)^{n/2}P_{n}\left(\frac{(a+b)c-2ab}{2\sqrt{ab(c-a)(c-b)}}\right)}{\sqrt{\pi}}

(F.6)

where
P_{n}(z)
are Legendre polynomials.

These coefficients
F_{n}
are rational functions of the ratios of eigenvalues
a,b,c
.
The first four coefficients

\displaystyle F_{0}=1,

(F.7)

\displaystyle F_{1}=\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{a}{c-a}+\frac{b}{c-b}\right),

(F.8)

\displaystyle F_{2}=\frac{3\left(c^{2}\left(3a^{2}+2ab+3b^{2}\right)+8a^{2}b^{2}-8abc(a+b)\right)}{32(a-c)^{2}(b-c)^{2}},

(F.9)

\displaystyle F_{3}=\frac{15(2ab-c(a+b))}{128(a-c)^{3}(c-b)^{3}}*

\displaystyle\left(c^{2}\left(5a^{2}-2ab+5b^{2}\right)+8a^{2}b^{2}-8abc(a+b)\right);

(F.10)

\displaystyle\dots

(F.11)

Appendix G
Geometric structure of constrained vortex surfaces
(by Camillo De Lellis, Elia Brué)

This short note derives some geometric conditions that Migdal’s constrained vortex surfaces have to satisfy.

G.1
CVS conditions: geometric interpretation and instability in the compact case

Let
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a (sufficiently smooth) complete connected surface. We denote by
\mathcal{S}_{-}
the region enclosed by
\mathcal{S}
, and set
\mathcal{S}_{+}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus(\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{S}_{-})
.

According to Migdal’s definition, a CVS (constrained vortex surface) solution to the Euler equations is a pair of (sufficiently smooth) velocity fields
(v_{-},v_{+})
defined on
\mathcal{S}_{-}
and
\mathcal{S}_{+}
, respectively, such that

\displaystyle{\rm div}\,v_{+}={\rm div}\,v_{-}=0

(G.1)

\displaystyle{\rm curl}\,v_{+}={\rm curl}\,v_{-}=0

(G.2)

\displaystyle v_{+}\,,v_{-}\quad

are tangent to
\mathcal{S}

(G.3)

\displaystyle(Dv_{+}+Dv_{-})

\displaystyle\cdot(v_{+}-v_{-})=0

(G.4)

\displaystyle|v_{+}|^{2}-|v_{-}|^{2}

is locally constant on
\mathcal{S}
.

(G.5)

Moreover, the constrained vortex surface has to satisfy the following stability condition

\langle D(v_{+}+v_{-})\cdot n\,,n\rangle<0\,,

(G.6)

where
n
is the exterior normal to
\mathcal{S}
and
\langle a,b\rangle
denotes the scalar product of the vectors
a
and
b
.

We have the following geometric characterization of (
G.3
), (
G.4
) and (
G.5
).

Theorem G.1
.

Conditions (
G.3
), (
G.4
), (
G.5
) are satisfied iff

\begin{cases}A(v_{-},v_{-})=A(v_{+},v_{+})\\
[v_{+},v_{-}]=0\,,\end{cases}

(G.7)

where
A
is the second fundamental form of
\mathcal{S}
, and
[v_{+},v_{-}]
is the Lie bracket, i.e.

[v_{+},v_{-}]=Dv_{+}\cdot v_{-}-Dv_{-}\cdot v_{+}\,.

Remark G.2
.

Recall that the Lie Bracket
[v_{+},v_{-}]=(Dv_{+}\cdot v_{-}-Dv_{-}\cdot v_{+})|_{\mathcal{S}}
is tangent to
\mathcal{S}
because
v_{+}
and
v_{-}
are tangent to
\mathcal{S}
.

Proof.

Condition (
G.5
) is equivalent to

(Dv_{+}\cdot v_{+}-Dv_{-}\cdot v_{-})|_{\mathcal{S}}\quad\mbox{is parallel to $n$}\,.

(G.8)

We rewrite (
G.4
) as
I+II=0
, where

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=Dv_{+}\cdot v_{+}-Dv_{-}\cdot v_{-}

\displaystyle II

\displaystyle=-Dv_{+}\cdot v_{-}+Dv_{-}\cdot v_{+}\,,

and observe that
I
is normal to
\mathcal{S}
, as a consequence of (
G.8
), while
II
is tangent to
\mathcal{S}
. Indeed,

\langle II,n\rangle=A(v_{+},v_{-})-A(v_{-},v_{+})=0\,.

In particular
I+II=0
iff
I=0
and
II=0
.
It is immediate to rewrite the two conditions as (
G.7
).

∎

Regarding the stability condition (
G.6
), we can prove the following rigidity result.

Theorem G.3
.

If
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a smooth compact closed surface and
v_{+},v_{-}
a pair of vector fields that satisfy the conditions (
G.1
) and (
G.3
), then

\intop\nolimits_{\mathcal{S}}\langle D(v_{+}+v_{-})\cdot n\,,n\rangle=0\,.

(G.9)

In particular, (
G.6
) cannot hold.

The same argument allows for a similar conclusion when
\mathcal{S}
is a smooth compact surface with boundary
\partial\mathcal{S}
and
v^{+}+v^{-}
is tangent to the boundary.

Theorem G.4
.

Assume
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a smooth compact surface with smooth boundary
\partial\mathcal{S}
and
v_{+},v_{-}
a pair of vector fields that satisfy the conditions (
G.1
) and (
G.3
). If in addition
v^{+}+v^{-}
is tangent to
\partial\mathcal{S}
, then

\intop\nolimits_{\mathcal{S}}\langle D(v_{+}+v_{-})\cdot n\,,n\rangle=0\,.

(G.10)

In particular, (
G.6
) cannot hold.

Remark G.5
.

Clearly Theorem
G.3
can be thought as the particular case of Theorem
G.4
has empty boundary.
The smoothness needed by the proof given below is that the surface and its boundary are both
C^{1}
(i.e. they have a tangent at every point, which in turn varies continuosly), and that the vector fields are
C^{1}
, i.e. continuously differentiable.

Proof of Theorem
G.4
.

Fix a point
p\in\mathcal{S}
and choose a local orthonormal frame around
p
consisting of
e_{1},e_{2},n
, where the vectors
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
are tangent to
\mathcal{S}
. Note that, since both
v_{+}
and
v_{-}
are divergence free we have that
D(v_{+}+v_{-})
is a trace-free matrix, and thus

\langle D(v_{+}+v_{-})\cdot n,n\rangle=-\langle D(v_{+}+v_{-})\cdot e_{1},e_{1}\rangle-\langle D(v_{+}+v_{-})\cdot e_{2},e_{2}\rangle\,.

(G.11)

Let
g
be the Riemannian scalar product induced on
\mathcal{S}
by the Euclidean one, and denote by
\nabla^{\mathcal{S}}
the corresponding Levi-Civita connection. Recall that the latter coincides with the connection induced by the Euclidean connection. This amounts to say that, if
X,Y
and
Z
are tangent vector fields to
\mathcal{S}
, then

g(\nabla^{\mathcal{S}}_{Y}X,Z)=\langle DX\cdot Y,Z\rangle\,.

If we apply this to
Y=Z=e_{i}
and
X=v_{+}+v_{-}
, (
G.11
) can be rewritten as

g(\nabla^{\mathcal{S}}_{e_{1}}(v_{+}+v_{-}),e_{1})+g(\nabla^{\mathcal{S}}_{e_{2}}(v_{+}+v_{-}),e_{2})=-\langle D(v_{+}+v_{-})\cdot n\,,n\rangle\,.

(G.12)

Notice now that the left hand side of (
G.12
) is the divergence in
\mathcal{S}
of the tangent vector field
v_{+}+v_{-}
, namely we have

{\rm div}_{\mathcal{S}}\,(v_{+}+v_{-})=-\langle D(v_{+}+v_{-})\cdot n\,,n\rangle\,.

(G.13)

Next, by Gauss theorem, the fact that
\mathcal{S}
is has a smooth boundary implies that

\intop\nolimits_{\mathcal{S}}{\rm div}_{\mathcal{S}}\,(v_{+}+v_{-})=-\intop\nolimits_{\partial\mathcal{S}}(v_{+}+v_{-})\cdot\nu\,,

(G.14)

where we denote by
\nu
the smooth unit vector field on
\partial\mathcal{S}
which is tangent to
\mathcal{S}
, orthogonal to
\partial\mathcal{S}
and points “inwards”, i.e. towards
\mathcal{S}
.
Since
v^{+}+v^{-}
is parallel to
\partial\mathcal{S}
, the integrand in the right hand side of (
G.14
) vanishes identically, which allows us to conclude (
G.10
).
∎

G.2
Rigidity of the CVS condition for closed surfaces

We prove rigidity results for closed connected CVS surfaces without imposing the stability condition (
G.6
). We prove that

(A)

there are no CVS solutions with
\mathcal{S}
homeomorphic to the sphere;

(B)

if
\mathcal{S}
has genus bigger than
1
, then
\mathcal{S}
cannot be real analytic;

(C)

if
\mathcal{S}
has genus
1
, then there are no axisymmetric solutions.

The precise formulation of (A) is the following:

Theorem G.6
.

If
(v_{-},v_{+})
satisfies the CVS conditions (
G.1
), (
G.2
), (
G.3
), (
G.4
), (
G.5
), and
\mathcal{S}
is homeomorphic to the sphere, then
v_{+}=0
and
v_{-}=0
.

Point (B) is a consequence of a more general fact which we state in the following theorem.

Theorem G.7
.

The following holds:

(i)

If
\mathcal{S}
is a smooth closed connected CVS surface with genus different than
1
, then
|v_{+}|^{2}=|v_{-}|^{2}
.

(ii)

If
\mathcal{S}
is any smooth CVS surface and
|v_{+}|^{2}=|v_{-}|^{2}
, then for any point
q\in\mathcal{S}
one of the following conditions must necessarily hold:

(a)

The Gauss curvature of
\mathcal{S}
is
0
at
q
;

(b)

v_{+}(q)=v_{-}(q)
;

(c)

v_{+}(q)=-v_{-}(q)
.

From the above theorem we draw the following simple

Corollary G.8
.

Assume
\mathcal{S}
is a closed smooth connected CVS surface with genus strictly higher than
1
. Then either
v_{+}=v_{-}
on some nontrivial open subset of
\mathcal{S}
, or
v_{+}=-v_{-}
on some nontrivial open subset of
\mathcal{S}
.

Proof.

In fact, if the sets
\{v_{+}=v_{-}\}
and
\{v_{+}=-v_{-}\}
contain no interior points, then the Gauss curvature would vanish on a dense set, and by continuity it must vanish anywhere. But it is well known that a surface with vanishing Gauss curvature cannot be closed. ∎

The condition is even more stringent if
\mathcal{S}
is real analytic, i.e. it can be described as the graph of a function with a converging Taylor series around any point, up to rotation of the coordinates.

Corollary G.9
.

Assume
\mathcal{S}
is a closed real analytic connected CVS surface with genus strictly higher than
1
. Then either
v_{+}=v_{-}
on
\mathcal{S}
, or
v_{+}=-v_{-}
on
\mathcal{S}
.

Proof.

Since both
v_{+}
and
v_{-}
are locally gradients of harmonic functions which satisfy the Neumann boundary condition, it turns out that their restrictions to
\mathcal{S}
are real analytic as well. But real analytic functions which vanish on a nontrivial open set, must vanish identically because
\mathcal{S}
is connected.
∎

We finally detail the non existence result (C) for axisymmetric solutions of genus
1
.
Let us consider a closed simple curve
[0,1]\ni t\to\gamma(t)=(\gamma_{r}(t),\gamma_{z}(t))\in(0,\infty)^{2}
, and the associated torus of rotation
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
, parametrized by

(t,\theta)\to(\gamma_{r}(t)\cos\theta,\gamma_{r}(t)\sin\theta,\gamma_{z}(t))\,.

(G.15)

We use polar coordinates
(x,y,z)=(r\cos\theta,r\sin\theta,z)
.
Recall that
\frac{\partial}{\partial r}
,
\frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}
,
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
is an orthonormal frame.

We look for CVS solutions with axisymmetry. The general Ansatz is the following.

\displaystyle v_{-}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\alpha\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}=\nabla\theta

\displaystyle v_{+}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=a(r,z)\frac{\partial}{\partial r}+b(r,z)\frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}+c(r,z)\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\,,

for some
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
C^{1}
functions
a
,
b
and
c
.

Theorem G.10
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be given by (
G.15
), for some simple closed curve
\gamma=(\gamma_{r},\gamma_{z})
of class
C^{2}
.
If
(v_{-},v_{+})
is an axisymmetric pair that satisfies the CVS conditions (
G.1
), (
G.2
), (
G.3
), (
G.4
), (
G.5
), then either
v_{+}=v_{-}
on
\mathcal{S}
, or
v_{+}=-v_{-}
on
\mathcal{S}
.

G.3
Proof of Theorem
G.6

Since
\mathcal{S}_{-}
is simply connected we can write
v_{-}=\nabla\Phi_{-}
for some harmonic function
\Phi_{-}
, whose gradient is tangent to
\mathcal{S}
. A simple integration by parts gives

\intop\nolimits_{\mathcal{S}_{-}}|v_{-}|^{2}\,dx=\intop\nolimits_{\mathcal{S}_{-}}|\nabla\Phi_{-}|^{2}\,dx=\intop\nolimits_{\mathcal{S}}\Phi_{-}\nabla\Phi_{-}\cdot n\,dx=0\,,

(G.16)

where
n
denotes the exterior normal to
\mathcal{S}
.
We deduce that
v_{-}=0
. Thanks to Theorem
G.7
(i) we deduce that
v_{+}=0
on
\mathcal{S}
.
Since
v_{+}
is harmonic, the unique continuation principle implies that
v_{+}
vanishes identically.

G.4
Proof of Theorem
G.7

Let us begin by proving (i).
A well-known theorem in topology implies that any tangent vector field to a closed surface
\mathcal{S}
with genus different than
1
must necessarily vanish at some point. This excludes that the constant in (
G.5
) is positive (because then
v_{+}
would never vanish) or negative (because then
v_{-}
would never vanish).

Let us pass to the proof of (ii).
The key ingredient is the following lemma, whose proof is postponed at the end of this section.

Lemma G.11
.

Let
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a smooth surface and let
U\subset\Sigma
be an open set. Assume the existence of smooth velocity fields
u,w
defined on
U
and tangent to
\Sigma
. If

(i)

u(p),w(p)\neq 0
and
g_{p}(v,w)=0
for any
p\in U
, where
g
is the metric induced by the ambient space
\mathbb{R}^{3}
;

(ii)

[u,w]=0
in
U
;

(iii)

u
and
v
are gradients in
U
, i.e. there exist
\alpha,\beta\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}U\to\mathbb{R}
such that
g_{p}(u,\cdot)={\rm d}_{p}\alpha
and
g_{p}(v,\cdot)={\rm d}_{p}\beta
for any
p\in U
.

Then, the Gaussian curvature of
\Sigma
is zero in
U
.

Let us explain how to prove Theorem
G.7
(ii) given Lemma
G.11
. Fix
q
such that
v_{+}(q)\neq v_{-}(q)
and
v_{+}(q)\neq v_{-}(q)
and let
U
be a neighborhood of
q
where
v_{+}-v_{-}
and
v_{+}+v_{-}
never vanish.
Since
|v_{+}|^{2}=|v_{-}|^{2}
, we have
g_{p}(u,w)=0
for any
p\in U
. Theorem
G.1
implies that
[v_{+},v_{-}]=0
, hence
[u,w]=0
in
U
. Moreover, (
G.2
) imply that
v_{+}
and
v_{-}
are irrotational in
\mathcal{S}_{+}
and
\mathcal{S}_{-}
, respectively, and (
G.3
) says that
v_{+}
and
v_{-}
are tangent to
\mathcal{S}
. This implies that,
v_{+}
and
v_{-}
are locally gradients in
\mathcal{S}
. In particular
u
and
w
are locally gradients in
U
. We are in position to apply Lemma
G.11
with
\Sigma=\mathcal{S}
, which implies that
K=0
in
U
, where
K
denotes the Gaussian curvature of
\mathcal{S}
. This implies in particular that the Gauss curvature of
\mathcal{S}
vanishes at
q
.

G.4.1
Proof of Lemma
G.11

Fix
p\in U
. We claim that there exists local coordinates around
p

X\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}W\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\to U\,,\quad(x,y)\to X(x,y)\in U\,,

such that

\frac{\partial}{\partial x}=u\,,\quad\frac{\partial}{\partial y}=w\,.

The claim follows from (ii), indeed we can define

X(x,y)=\phi_{u}^{x}\circ\phi_{w}^{y}(p)\in U\,,

where
\phi_{u}
and
\phi_{w}
are the flow maps associated to
u
and
v
, respectively. Observe that
X(0,0)=p
. Condition (ii) implies that the flow maps commute, hence

\frac{\partial}{\partial x}X(x,y)=u\circ X(x,y)\,,\quad\frac{\partial}{\partial y}X(x,y)=w\circ X(x,y)\,,

(G.17)

in particular (i), along with the implicit function theorem, says that
X
is a local parametrization in a neighborhood of
p
.

Let
h
be the metric in the new coordinates
(x,y)
. It turns out that

h_{x,y}=h_{y,x}=g\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x},\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\right)=g(u,w)=0

(G.18)

as a consequence of the previous claim and condition (i). We claim that

\frac{\partial}{\partial y}h_{x,x}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}h_{y,y}=0\,.

(G.19)

It immediately implies that
\Sigma
is flat in a neighborhood of
p
. Indeed, as a consequence of (
G.19
) we can write the metric as

h=a(x)dx^{2}+b(y)dy^{2}\,,

which is clearly flat.

Let us prove (
G.19
). As a consequence of (iii) we get

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial y}h_{x,x}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}g\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x},\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\right)=2g\left(\nabla_{\frac{\partial}{\partial y}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x},\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\right)

\displaystyle=2{\rm Hess}\,\alpha\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial y},\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\right)

(G.20)

the symmetry of the Hessian implies

{\rm Hess}\,\alpha\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial y},\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\right)={\rm Hess}\,\alpha\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x},\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\right)=g\left(\nabla_{\frac{\partial}{\partial x}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x},\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\right)\,,

(G.21)

on the other hand we know that
g\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x},\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\right)=0
, hence

\displaystyle g\left(\nabla_{\frac{\partial}{\partial x}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x},\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}g\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x},\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\right)-g\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x},\nabla_{\frac{\partial}{\partial x}}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\right)

\displaystyle=-g\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x},\nabla_{\frac{\partial}{\partial y}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\right)\,,

(G.22)

where in the last step we used that
\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial x},\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\right]=0
. By collection (
G.20
), (
G.21
), and (
G.22
) we deduce

\frac{\partial}{\partial y}h_{x,x}=-\frac{\partial}{\partial y}h_{x,x}\,,

which implies our claim. We argue in the same way to show that
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}h_{y,y}=0
.

G.5
Proof of Theorem
G.10

Without loss of generality we assume that
\gamma\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[0,L]\to(0,\infty)^{2}
is parametrized by arclength, where
L
is the length of
\gamma
.
Recall that we look for solutions of the form

\displaystyle v_{-}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\alpha\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}

\displaystyle v_{+}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=a(r,z)\frac{\partial}{\partial r}+b(r,z)\frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}+c(r,z)\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\,,

where
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
a
,
b
,
c
are
C^{1}
functions.

Lemma G.12
.

We have that

{\rm curl}\,v_{+}=0
if and only if
b(r,z)r
is constant and

{\rm curl}\,\left(a(r,z)\frac{\partial}{\partial r}+c(r,z)\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\right)=0\,.

(G.23)

Proof.

We compute

{\rm curl}\,v_{+}=\left(\frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial c}{\partial\theta}-\frac{\partial b}{\partial z}\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial r}+\left(\frac{\partial a}{\partial z}-\frac{\partial c}{\partial r}\right)\frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}+\frac{1}{r}\left(\frac{\partial(rb)}{\partial r}-\frac{\partial a}{\partial\theta}\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\,.

Since
v_{+}
is axisymmetric, we have that
{\rm curl}\,v_{+}=0
if and only if

\begin{cases}\frac{\partial b}{\partial z}=0\\
\frac{\partial(rb)}{\partial r}=0\\
{\rm curl}\left(a\frac{\partial}{\partial r}+c\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\right)=0\,.\end{cases}

(G.24)

The first two conditions amount to
br=C
, for some constant
C\in\mathbb{R}
. The latter amounts to (
G.23
).
∎

Let us begin by considering the case
\alpha\neq 0
. Without loss of generality we can assume
\alpha=1
.
After imposing
{\rm curl}\,v_{+}=0
, we can write

v_{+}=a(r,z)\frac{\partial}{\partial r}+c(r,z)\frac{\partial}{\partial z}+Cv_{-}=\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}w_{+}+Cv_{-}\,.

(G.25)

To satisfy the CVS conditions we need to impose the following properties on
w_{+}
:

(1)

{\rm div}\,w_{+}={\rm curl}\,w_{+}=0

(2)

w_{+}
is tangent to
\mathcal{S}

(3)

|w_{+}|^{2}+\frac{C^{2}-1}{r^{2}}=\ell
in
\mathcal{S}
, for some
\ell\in\mathbb{R}

(4)

[w_{+},v_{-}]=0
in
\mathcal{S}

(5)

A(w_{+}+Cv_{-},w_{+}+Cv_{-})=A(v_{-},v_{-})

We show that (2), (3) and (4) imply that
\gamma_{r}
is constant.

Condition (4).

We compute

\displaystyle[w_{+},v_{-}]

\displaystyle=\nabla_{w_{+}}v_{-}-\nabla_{v_{-}}w_{+}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{a\frac{\partial}{\partial r}+c\frac{\partial}{\partial z}}\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\right)-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\nabla_{\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}}\left(a\frac{\partial}{\partial r}+c\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\right)=-\frac{2}{r^{3}}a\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\,.

Hence, we have to impose

a(\gamma_{r}(t),\gamma_{z}(t))=0\,,\quad\text{for any $t\in[0,L]$}\,.

(G.26)

Condition (2) and (3).
Recall that

n=(\dot{\gamma}_{z}(t)\cos\theta,\dot{\gamma}_{z}(t)\sin\theta,-\dot{\gamma}_{r}(t))\,,

where
n
denotes the exterior normal to
\mathcal{S}
.
By using (
G.26
), we deduce

w_{+}=c(r,z)\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\,,\quad\text{on $\mathcal{S}$}\,,

(G.27)

hence

0=w_{+}\cdot n=-c(\gamma_{r}(t),\gamma_{z}(t))\,\dot{\gamma}_{r}(t)\,.

In particular, by employing (
G.27
) and (3) we get

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=|c(\gamma_{r}(t),\gamma_{z}(t))|^{2}|\dot{\gamma}_{r}(r)|^{2}=|w_{+}|^{2}|\dot{\gamma}_{r}(t)|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\ell+\frac{1-C^{2}}{\gamma_{r}^{2}(t)}\right)|\dot{\gamma}_{r}(t)|^{2}\,.

If either
C^{2}\neq 1
or
\ell\neq 0
, then
\gamma_{r}={\rm const}
. This is impossible because
\gamma
is a closed simple curved.

If
C^{2}=1
and
\ell=0
, then (3) implies that
|w_{+}|^{2}=0
on
\mathcal{S}
. It amounts to
v_{+}=v_{-}
when
C=1
and
v_{+}=-v_{-}
when
C=-1
.

The case
\alpha=0
.

Let us now assume that
\alpha=0
.
By applying Lemma
G.12
we deduce

v_{+}=a(r,z)\frac{\partial}{\partial r}+c(r,z)\frac{\partial}{\partial z}+C\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}=\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}w_{+}+C\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\,.

In this case, to satisfy the CVS conditions we need to impose the following properties on
w_{+}
:

(1’)

{\rm div}\,w_{+}={\rm curl}\,w_{+}=0

(2’)

w_{+}
is tangent to
\mathcal{S}

(3’)

|w_{+}|^{2}+\frac{C^{2}}{r^{2}}=\ell
in
\mathcal{S}
, for some
\ell\in\mathbb{R}

(4’)

A(w_{+}+C\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta},w_{+}+C\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta})=0
.

We show that (4’) forces
C=0
.
Since
w_{+}
is tangent to
\mathcal{S}
, there exists
\lambda\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

w_{+}(\gamma)=\lambda\left(\dot{\gamma}_{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}+\dot{\gamma}_{z}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\right)\,.

(G.28)

We use the well-known identities

\displaystyle A\left(\dot{\gamma}_{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}+\dot{\gamma}_{z}\frac{\partial}{\partial z},\dot{\gamma}_{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}+\dot{\gamma}_{z}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\right)

\displaystyle=\kappa

\displaystyle A\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta},\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=\gamma_{r}\dot{\gamma}_{z}

\displaystyle A\left(\dot{\gamma}_{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}+\dot{\gamma}_{z}\frac{\partial}{\partial z},\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=0\,,

where
\kappa=\ddot{\gamma}_{z}\dot{\gamma}_{r}-\ddot{\gamma}_{r}\dot{\gamma}_{z}
is the curvature of
\gamma
.
We deduce

0=A\left(w_{+}+C\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta},w_{+}+C\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\right)=\lambda^{2}\kappa+\frac{C^{2}}{\gamma_{r}^{3}}\dot{\gamma}_{z}

(G.29)

Let us consider
t_{0}\in[0,L]
, a minimum point for
\gamma_{r}
. Since
\dot{\gamma}_{r}(t_{0})=0
and
\gamma
is parametrized by arclength, we deduce that
\dot{\gamma}_{z}(t_{0})\neq 0
. Hence, (
G.29
) implies that
\lambda(t_{0})\neq 0
. So, in a small neighborhood of
t_{0}
we can rewrite (
G.29
) as

\ddot{\gamma}_{z}\dot{\gamma}_{r}-\ddot{\gamma}_{r}\dot{\gamma}_{z}=\kappa=\frac{C^{2}}{\lambda^{2}\gamma_{r}^{3}}\dot{\gamma}_{z}\,.

We multiply the latter by
\dot{\gamma}_{z}
, and use the identity
\ddot{\gamma}_{z}\dot{\gamma}_{z}=-\ddot{\gamma}_{r}\dot{\gamma}_{r}
(which is a consequence of
(\dot{\gamma}_{r})^{2}+(\dot{\gamma}_{z})^{2}=1
), to get

\ddot{\gamma}_{r}=-\frac{C^{2}}{\lambda^{2}\gamma_{r}^{3}}(1-(\dot{\gamma}_{r})^{2})\,.

(G.30)

Using that
\ddot{\gamma}_{r}(t_{0})\geq 0
and
\dot{\gamma}_{r}(t_{0})=0
we conclude that
C=0
.

We now use (3’) to deduce that
v_{+}=0
. Indeed,
\lambda^{2}=\ell
, since
\lambda
is continuous we deduce that
\lambda
is a constant. If
\lambda=0
then
v_{+}=0
.
If
\lambda\neq 0
, then (
G.30
) gives
\kappa=0
, which contradicts the fact that
\gamma
is closed.

G.6
Solutions with cylindrical symmetry

In
[
8
]
Migdal finds stable solutions to the CVS equations with linear growth and
vorticity concentrated on a cylinder
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
It turns out that the cross section of
\mathcal{S}
is noncompact. Given the cylindrical symmetry and the linear growth at infinity, that is the best one can hope for. Below we show that there are
no
solutions to the CVS equations (irrespectively of the stability condition) with cylindrical symmetry, compact cross section and linear growth.

Consider:

(i)

a smooth simple closed curve
\sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
;

(ii)

the bounded simply connected domain
\Omega_{-}
bounded by
\sigma
;

(iii)

the unbounded domain
\Omega_{+}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus(\sigma\cup\Omega_{-})
;

(iv)

the surface
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
given by
\sigma\times\mathbb{R}
;

(v)

the cylindrical domains
\mathcal{S}_{\pm}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\Omega_{\pm}\times\mathbb{R}
.

We are looking for two bounded vector fields
v_{\pm}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Omega_{\pm}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
and a quadratic function
q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
with the following properties:

(a)

q
is harmonic;

(b)

the maps
u_{\pm}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(v_{\pm},0)+\nabla q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{S}_{\pm}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
are divergence free, curl-free and tangent to
\mathcal{S}
;

(c)

for any point
p\in\mathcal{S}
, the vector
u_{+}(p)-u_{-}(p)
belongs to the kernel of
Du_{+}(p)+Du_{-}(p)
.

We claim the following.

Theorem G.13
.

v_{\pm}
and
q
must vanish identically.

We can in fact consider the following more general situation:

(i’)

\sigma_{i}
,
i\in\{1,\ldots,N_{0}\}
, is an arbitrary finite collection of simple closed curves which are pairwise disjoint;

(ii’)

\{\Omega_{j}\}
,
j\in\{1,\ldots,N_{0}+1\}
are the connected components of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\bigcup_{i}\sigma_{i}
;

(iii’)

The surface
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
is the union of the cylinders
\mathcal{S}_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sigma_{i}\times\mathbb{R}
;

(iv’)

The cylindrical domains are given by
\Lambda_{j}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\Omega_{j}\times\mathbb{R}
.

Under these more general assumptions we are looking for
N_{0}+1
bounded vector fields
v_{j}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Omega_{j}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
and a quadratic function
q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
with the following properties:

(a’)

q
is harmonic;

(b’)

The maps
u_{j}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(v_{j},0)+\nabla q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Lambda_{j}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
are divergence-free, curl-free, and tangent to
\partial\Lambda_{j}\subset\mathcal{S}
;

(c’)

If
\Lambda_{i}
and
\Lambda_{j}
have a common boundary
\mathcal{S}_{k}
, then for any point
p\in\mathcal{S}_{k}
the vector
u_{i}(p)-u_{j}(p)
belongs to the kernel of
Du_{i}(p)+Du_{j}(p)
.

Under these assumptions Theorem
G.13
can be generalized to

Theorem G.14
.

v_{i}
and
q
must vanish identically.

Proof of Theorem
G.13
.

Consider the function
\varphi(x,y)=q(x,y,0)
and the vector field
\xi_{-}(x,y)=v_{-}(x,y)+\nabla\varphi(x,y)
. Observe that
v_{-}
is curl-free and, since
\Omega_{-}
is simply connected, there is a potential
\zeta_{-}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Omega_{-}\to\mathbb{R}
for
\xi_{-}
. Now,

\frac{\partial\zeta_{-}}{\partial\nu}=0\qquad\mbox{on $\sigma=\partial\Omega_{-}$.}

(G.31)

On the other hand, since
\varphi
is quadratic,
\Delta\zeta_{-}
is a constant. Observe that, therefore, from

\intop\nolimits_{\Omega_{-}}\Delta\zeta_{-}=\intop\nolimits_{\sigma}\frac{\partial\zeta_{-}}{\partial\nu}=0\,,

(G.32)

it turns out that

\Delta\zeta_{-}=\Delta\varphi=0\,.

(G.33)

But then we can integrate by parts to conclude

\intop\nolimits_{\Omega_{-}}|\nabla\zeta_{-}|^{2}=\intop\nolimits_{\sigma}\zeta_{-}\frac{\partial\zeta_{-}}{\partial\nu}=0\,.

(G.34)

In particular
\xi_{-}
vanishes identically.

Define next
\xi_{+}(x,y)=v_{+}(x,y)+\nabla\varphi(x,y)
. (c) implies that
\xi_{+}(x,y)
is in the kernel of
D\xi_{+}(x,y)
for every
(x,y)\in\sigma
. Assume
\zeta_{+}
is a potential for
\xi_{+}
in some simply connected domain
U\cap\Omega_{+}
, where
U
is the neighborhood of some point
(x,y)\in\sigma
. Then the latter condition can be rewritten as

\frac{1}{2}\nabla|\nabla\zeta_{+}|^{2}=0\quad\text{on $\sigma\cap U$}\,.

If
|\nabla\zeta_{+}|=0
on
\sigma\cap U
, then
\zeta_{+}
is a constant over
\sigma\cap U
and if we extend
\zeta_{+}
to
\Omega_{-}\cap U
by setting it equal to the latter constant, we immediately see that
\zeta_{+}
is
C^{1}
on
U
and weakly harmonic, hence harmonic. So
\zeta_{+}
must vanish by unique continuation for harmonic functions. If
|\nabla\zeta_{+}|=c>0
, we conclude that
D^{2}\zeta_{+}(p)
has a nontrivial kernel for every
p\in\sigma\cap U
, but since the trace of the two-dimensional matrix
D^{2}\zeta_{+}(p)
is zero, we must conclude that
D^{2}\zeta_{+}
vanishes identically on
\sigma\cap U
. But this means that
\frac{\partial\zeta_{+}}{\partial x}
and
\frac{\partial\zeta_{+}}{\partial y}
are both locally constant over
\sigma\cap U
. The assumption that
\frac{\partial\zeta_{+}}{\partial\nu}=0
on
\sigma\cap U
implies therefore that
\nabla\zeta_{+}
must vanish identically on
\sigma\cap U
.

Having concluded that both
\xi_{-}
and
\xi_{+}
vanish identically, we immediately conclude that actually
u_{+}=u_{-}
on
\mathcal{S}
and that the corresponding function
u
given by defining
u=u_{\pm}
on
\mathcal{S}_{\pm}
is the gradient of a quadratic harmonic function.
Since
u
must be tangent to
\mathcal{S}
, we see right away that
u
must vanish identically.
∎

Proof of Theorem
G.14
.

The proof is by induction over the number
N_{0}
of curves. The start of the the induction, namely
N_{0}=1
, is in fact Theorem
G.13
. Consider therefore an arbitrary
N_{0}>1
and assume that the theorem is correct when
N_{0}
is substituted by
N_{0}-1
. Fix a collection of curves
\sigma_{i}
as in (i’) above. Each
\sigma_{i}
bounds a unique simply connected domain
\Xi_{i}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and given that the curves are pairwise disjoint, each
\sigma_{j}
with
j\neq i
is either contained in
\Xi_{i}
or in the interior of its complement. Since the curves are finitely many it is obvious that one of them is an “innermost” curve, namely there is a
\Xi_{i}
which does not contain any curve
\sigma_{j}
. Without loss of generality we can assume
i=1
and observe that
\Xi_{1}
must be one of the domains
\Omega_{i}
: again without loss of generality we can assume it is
\Omega_{1}
. We then denote by
\Omega_{2}
the only other connected component of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\bigcup_{i}\sigma_{i}
whose boundary intersects
\sigma_{1}
(in fact we must have
\sigma_{1}\subset\partial\Omega_{2}
, but observe that the inclusion might be strict). If we set
v_{-}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=v_{1}
and
v_{+}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=v_{2}
, we can now repeat the argument of Theorem
G.13
. First of all the potential
\zeta_{-}
exists in our case as well because
\Omega_{1}
is simply connected, and hence the conclusions (
G.31
)-(
G.32
) can all be drawn in our case as well. The subsequent argument leads to the conclusion that
v^{+}
is in fact a smooth continuation of
v^{-}
across
\sigma_{1}\cap U
in any simply connected neighborhood
U
of
p\in\sigma=\sigma_{1}
: the argument can be taken verbatim in our case as long as
U
does not intersect any other curve
\sigma_{j}
with
j>1
. In particular we conclude that
\sigma_{1}
could actually be eliminated from the collection of curves because the function
\tilde{v}
defined to be
v_{1}
in
\Omega_{1}
and
v_{2}
in
\Omega_{2}
is in fact smooth across
\sigma_{1}
. Having reduced the number of curves by
1
we can apply the inductive assumption and conclude the validity of the theorem.
∎

Appendix H
Topological defects in Turbulent flows,
(by Grigory Volovik)

Let us consider the Migdal view on classical turbulence (see Refs.
[
5
,
6
]
) and look for the connections between the classical hydrodynamics and turbulence in liquids and quantum hydrodynamics and turbulence in fermionic superfluids.

H.1
Classical vs quantum turbulence

Of course, there are important differences between the turbulence in quantum and classical systems.
In classical liquids, the viscous term has more derivatives that nonlinear Euler term. In fermionic superfluids both terms have the same number of derivatives,
see Eq.(2) in Ref.
[
55
]
. The corresponding Reynolds number is the ratio of two dimensionless parameters – the internal parameters of the liquid, reactive coefficient
1-\alpha^{\prime}
and the dissipative one
\alpha
.
This Reynolds number does not depend on velocity: it depends only on such internal characteristic of the liquid as temperature
T
. By changing
T
, the transition to quantum turbulence is experimentally observed, when the ratio of the reactive and dissipative parameters crosses unity.

The possible relation of viscosity anomaly to chiral anomaly may follow from the observation that the parameters
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
in Eq.(2) of Ref.
[
55
]
are related to the Adler-Bell-Jackiw (ABJ) chiral anomaly. The vortex has chirality, and its motion produces the spectral flow in the vortex cores, which is similar to that in ABJ anomaly and which in some limit case is described by the ABJ equation.
[
56
]
In that limit, which takes place for vorticity in chiral superfluid
3
He-A, the ABJ equation with the proper prefactor has been experimentally confirmed.
[
57
]

H.2
Clebsch hydrodynamics vs Heisenberg ferromagnets

The velocity field in Clebsch variable
(\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\phi_{3})
is
v_{i}=-\phi_{2}\nabla_{i}\phi_{1}+\nabla_{i}\phi_{3}
. In the Migdal theory, velocity and vorticity are determined in the compact space
S^{2}
, see Eq.(43) for vorticity in terms of the unit vector
\hat{\bf S}
:

\omega_{ik}=(\nabla\times{\bf v})_{ik}=Z(\hat{\bf S}\cdot(\nabla_{i}\hat{\bf S}\times\nabla_{k}\hat{\bf S}))\ \,.

(H.1)

The polar and azimuthal components of the unit vector
\hat{\bf S}
are

\phi_{1}/Z=\cos\theta
,
\phi_{1}=\phi
, while
Z
is adiabatic invariant in the absence of viscosity.

Eq.(
H.1
) also takes place in chiral superfluid
3
He-A, where vorticity is expressed in terms of the unit vector of the orbital angular momentum
\hat{\bf l}\equiv\hat{\bf S}
.
In this superfluid the prefactor
Z
is fixed,
Z=\hbar/4m_{3}
, where
m_{3}
is the mass of the
3
He atom,
[
58
,
56
]
and this equation is known as the Mermin-Ho relation. Since in the chiral superfluid
\kappa=\pi\hbar/m_{3}
is the the quantum of circulation, the prefactor
Z
in Migdal hydrodynamics plays the role of the circulation quantum, see Sec.
H.4
.

However, the Clebsch fields are not well determined, since
\phi_{2}
is not defined at
\theta=0
. Actually these Clebsch variables are exactly the same as used in ferromagnets, where the magnetization is
{\bf M}=M\hat{\bf m}\equiv Z\hat{\bf S}
,
M-M_{z}=M(1-\cos\beta)\equiv Z(1-\cos\beta)=\phi_{1}
, and
\phi\equiv\phi_{2}
. Here
\beta
is the angle of deflection of magnetization from the
z
-axis, and
\phi
is the azimuthal angle of magnetization. The role of vorticity is played by the effective gauge field
F_{ik}\equiv\omega_{ik}
acting on electrons from magnetization.
[
59
]
The pseudoelectric field
F_{i0}\equiv\omega_{i0}
gives rise to the so-called spin-motive force acting on electrons. These gauge fields are related to the Berry phase.

In ferromagnets,
\phi
and
M_{z}
(or
M-M_{z}
, since
M
is the dynamical invariant) are canonically conjugate variables. But since
\phi\equiv\phi_{2}
is not well determined, the
3+1
action
\intop\nolimits dtdVM_{z}\dot{\phi}
is substituted by the
4+1
Wess-Zumino term,
\intop\nolimits dtd\tau dVF_{0\tau}
, see Eq.(19) in Ref.
[
59
]
.
Probably such approach is to be used in Migdal theory.

One may say that the big difference with ferromagnets is that there the magnetization is observable, while in hydrodynamics the Clebsch variables are not. However, if the spin-orbit interaction is neglected, the magnetization becomes unobservable.
But the effective gauge invariant gauge field
F_{\mu\nu}
acting on fermions in ferromagnets will be observable in the same way as its analog – gauge invariant vorticity
\omega_{\mu\nu}
, which is expressed in terms of the unit vector
\hat{\bf S}
of the analog of magnetization.

H.3
Topological objects: hedgehog, instanton, domain wall, quantum of circulation

Since
M\equiv Z
is the dynamical invariant in reversible dynamics, the reversible hydrodynamics is fully determined by the unit vector of magnetization
\hat{\bf m}\equiv\hat{\bf S}
. It has two topological invariants
\pi_{2}
and
\pi_{3}
.

The
\pi_{2}
invariant is
N=\intop\nolimits\,dS_{i}\omega^{i}/Z
. It is proportional to the velocity circulation along the moving loop, which is conserved in moving liquid in the absence of dissipation. For the closed surface, the invariant
N
should be integer describing the topological defects – the hedgehogs or monopoles. In ferromagnets, where
\hat{\bf m}
is the original variable and vorticity (gauge field
\omega_{ik}
) is the secondary variable, the hedgehog in
\hat{\bf m}
field looks as the Dirac monopole in the Berry phase, with the unobservable Dirac line, at which the Berry phase changes by
2\pi
.

In hydrodynamics, where vorticity
\omega_{ik}
is the original variable and
\hat{\bf S}
is the secondary one, the hedgehogs in the
\hat{\bf S}
field are impossible as separate objects. The Dirac line attached to such monopole is singular and is observable. The same is in the chiral superfluids, where the Dirac line is the singular doubly quantized vortex,
m=2
(or
m=2N
in general case, where
N
is the topological charge of the monopole). Nevertheless, in Migdal theory the integer
m
is important, because the circulation
\Gamma=Zm
, and thus the prefactor
Z
plays the role of circulation quantum, and
m
is the number of quanta.

The
\pi_{3}
invariant is the Hopf invariant, which is proportional to the integral over helicity:
N_{3}=\intop\nolimits d^{3}r\,{\bf v}\cdot{\bm{\omega}}/Z^{2}
. The corresponding topological defect is instanton, which describes the process of creation of helicity from the "vacuum".

In Heisenberg ferromagnets there are no domain walls, because
\pi_{0}=0
. In hydrodynamics the surfaces are possible at which the prefactor
Z
changes sign. Probably the vortex sheets in Migdal theory – the thin pancake-like regions of
increasing vorticity
[
60
]
are the analogous objects.

H.4
Dynamic invariants in hydrodynamics (circulation and helicity) and their quantization in superfluids

In Migdal theory, the circulation
\Gamma=Zm
is the dynamic invariant. In superfluids, the quantity
m
becomes the integer number, i.e. the circulation is quantized:
Z=2\pi\hbar/m_{4}
and
Z=\pi\hbar/m_{3}
is the quantum of circulations in Bose superfluid
4
He with mass of atom
m_{4}
and in Fermi superfluid
3
He with mass of atom
m_{3}
correspondingly, and
m
is the number circulation quanta.
Thus in Migdal theory, the prefactor
Z
plays the role of circulation quantum, and
m
plays the role of the number of quanta, which becomes integer in the quantum case.

In the same manner, in superfluids the integral of helicity is quantized,
H=Z^{2}N_{3}
with integer
N_{3}
. The integer
N_{3}
in superfluids is the topological invariant: it is the knot invariant in Bose superfluid
4
He and in Fermi superfluid
3
He-B, and the Hopf invariant
\pi_{3}(S^{2})
in the chiral superfluid
3
He-A. In both Fermi superfluids, the quantum of helicity is
Z^{2}\propto(\hbar/m_{3})^{2}
. In classical hydrodynamics, the prefactor
Z^{2}\propto(\hbar/m_{3})^{2}
serves as the "quantum of helicity", and
N_{3}
serves as the "number of quanta", which becomes the integer topological invariant in the quantum case of superfluids.

The quantization of helicity and of circulation in superfluids provide two examples, which illustrate the main property of the dynamical invariants in classical systems. The dynamic invariant in classical systems become quantized in integer numbers in the quantum systems,
N_{3}
and
N
correspondingly.

Similar situation is in Heisenberg ferromagnets. In classical equations
M\equiv Z
is the dynamic invariant, which is conserved in the Landau–Lifshitz equation without dissipation. In ferromagnets the total magnetic moment
\intop\nolimits d^{3}xM
becomes quantized in terms of
\hbar/2
, i.e.

\intop\nolimits d^{3}xM=k\hbar/2
, where
k
is integer. This quantization follows from the Wess-Zumino term in action.
[
61
]

H.5
Hydrodynamics in terms of tetrads

Let us introduce the orthogonal triad of unit length:

{\bf e}_{1}\,\,,\,{\bf e}_{2}\,\,,\,\hat{\bf S}={\bf e}_{1}\times{\bf e}_{2}\,.

(H.2)

The velocity field:

v_{i}=Z({\bf e}_{1}\cdot\nabla_{i}{\bf e}_{2}-{\bf e}_{2}\cdot\nabla_{i}{\bf e}_{1})\ \,,

(H.3)

where
Z
is the dynamical invariant.
The vorticity is expressed in terms
\hat{\bf S}
field (according to the Mermin-Ho relation) in the same way as in Migdal theory in Eq.(43) of Ref.
[
5
]
:

\omega_{ik}=(\nabla\times{\bf v})_{ik}=Z(\hat{\bf S}\cdot(\nabla_{i}\hat{\bf S}\times\nabla_{k}\hat{\bf S}))\ \,.

(H.4)

The triad field variables correspond to three Clebsch fields, but as distinct from the Clebsch fields the triad is well determined everywhere, except for the real topological singularities.

The
SO(3)
space of triad, and
S^{2}
space of
\hat{\bf S}
provide the geometry, including the effective metric, and topology.
The topological invariants are well determined in terms of
\hat{\bf S}
, or in terms of rotation of the dyad about
\hat{\bf S}
-axis. They are integer for special geometries, while the circulation and helicity are represented by these invariants multiplied by the dynamical invariants
Z
and
Z^{2}
correspondingly.

H.6
Dirac monopole with observable Dirac strings

The hydrodynamics of incompressible fluid has been discussed in terms of the
{\bf S}
-vector in Ref.
[
62
]
.
The unit vector description allows the topological objects
\pi_{3}(S^{2})=Z
, which describe the Hopf structure, and also the instantons. It also allows the defects of the group
\pi_{2}(S^{2})=Z
, the hedgehogs, or monopoles, in which the
{\bf S}
-vector wraps
N
times around a sphere. In tetrads, where the target space is
SO(3)
, the Hopf structures are allowed, since
\pi_{3}(SO(3))=Z
. But the hedgehogs do not exist as separate objects, since
\pi_{2}(SO(3))=0
. That is why the monopole in
{\bf S}
-field has singular Dirac string (the vortex singularity)
attached to the monopole, which is observable.
[
63
,
64
,
65
]

The simplest monopole is the radial hedgehog in the
\hat{\bf S}
field:
\hat{\bf S}=\hat{\bf r}
.
Its topological
\pi_{2}
charge is
N=1
. Let us consider two configurations of
N=1
monopole: (i) with one Dirac string with winding number
m=2N=2
; and (ii) with two strings, each with winding number
m=1
.

H.7
Dirac monopole with one Dirac string

(z,\rho,\phi)
are cylindrical coordinates and
(r,\theta,\phi)
are spherical coordinates.
The velocity field is

{\bf v}({\bf r})=Z{\hat{\bm{\phi}}}\frac{1-\cos\theta}{r\sin\theta}\,\,,\,\,\nabla\cdot{\bf v}=0\,,

(H.5)

and vorticity field:

{\bm{\omega}}({\bf r})=Z\frac{\hat{\bf r}}{r^{2}}+2Z\theta(-z){\hat{\bf z}}\delta_{2}({\bm{\rho}})\,\,,\,\,\nabla\cdot{\bm{\omega}}=0\,.

(H.6)

The vorticity is equivalent to magnetic field of Dirac monopole with charge
Z
, i.e.
{\bf B}\equiv{\bm{\omega}}({\bf r})
. The magnetic flux is conserved,
\nabla\cdot{\bf B}=0
. The
4\pi Z
flux from the monopole is compensated by the magnetic flux along the singular flux tube (observable Dirac string) to the monopole. Here there is the single Dirac string on the lower half-axis,
z<0
. It is the vortex line with circulation
2Z
, or winding number
m=2
in Migdal notations.
The triads are

\hat{\bf S}({\bf r})=\hat{\bf r}\,\,,\,{\bf e}_{1}+i{\bf e}_{2}=e^{i\phi}({\hat{\bm{\theta}}}+i{\hat{\bm{\phi}}})\,.

(H.7)

This topological structure with vortex terminated by monopole may have some connection to tornado, see Figs.
38
and
39
. Here the funnel cloud moves towards the ground and when it reaches the ground it forms tornado in Fig.
40
. Before the tornado is formed the funnel cloud is the vortex with the end point. The end point represents the monopole-hedgehog in the
\hat{\bf S}
-field and the funnel cloud represents its Dirac string. Probably the monopole may exist also at the final stage of the tornado: when tornado is dying the end of the vortex leaves the ground.

H.8
Dirac monopole with two Dirac strings

In this case the velocity field is:

{\bf v}({\bf r})=-Z{\hat{\bm{\phi}}}\frac{\cos\theta}{r\sin\theta}\,,

(H.8)

and vorticity field:

{\bm{\omega}}({\bf r})=Z\frac{\hat{\bf r}}{r^{2}}+Z\,\sign(z)\,\hat{\bf z}\delta_{2}({\bm{\rho}})\,.

(H.9)

Now the flux from the monopole is compensated by the magnetic fluxes which enter the monopole along two Dirac strings.
Each string (vortex line) has circulation
Z
, or winding number
m=1
in Migdal notations.
The triads are

\hat{\bf S}({\bf r})=\hat{\bf r}\,\,,\,{\bf e}_{1}+i{\bf e}_{2}={\hat{\bm{\theta}}}+i{\hat{\bm{\phi}}}\,.

(H.10)

H.9
General case with two Dirac strings and monopole with charge N

The monopole in
{\bf S}
-field with topological charge
N
, has the Dirac strings with the total winding number
m=2N
.
The triads can be chosen as extension of
m=1
monopole in Eq.(
H.10
):

{\bf e}_{1}+i{\bf e}_{2}=({\hat{\bm{\theta}}}+i{\hat{\bm{\phi}}})^{N}\,.

(H.11)

There are two Dirac strings, at
z<0
and at
z>0
, each with winding number
m=N
(the total winding number is
m=2N
).
It is not clear whether the monopoles with
|N|>1
could be realized.

H.10
Monopole in spherical layer

The monopole configuration with
N=1
with two strings, each with
m=1
, is realized for hydrodynamics of ideal incompressible fluid in a thin spherical layer.
[
66
]
Here
{\bf S}=\hat{\bf r}
, and thus the topological charge of the monopole is
N=1
.
The first term in the RHS of Eq.(
H.9
) can be neglected in the limit of large radius of the sphere. Then the velocity field satisfies both stationary equations for incompressible fluid:

\nabla\cdot{\bf v}=0\,\,,\,\,\nabla\times({\bf v}\times{\bm{\omega}})=0\,.

(H.12)

This is the monopole, which core is represented by the inner sphere.

Figure 38:
Since the end point of the vortex is not seen, this is probably the funnel cloud, which is the vortex (Dirac string) terminated by the Dirac monopole.

Figure 39:
One more monopole.

Figure 40:
Tornado can be represented as Dirac string, while the spherical core of the Dirac monopole is represented by the Earth.

The simplest realization is tornado in Figs.
40
and
41
, where the Earth represents the core of the monopole.

Figure 41:
One more Dirac string

Figure 42:
Dirac strings again

Viscosity resolves the singularity on Dirac line. This configuration can be also described in terms of
\hat{\bf S}
-vector:

\hat{\bf S}({\bf r})
is vertical outside the tornado and becomes radial on the axis.
The simplest model for that is the Rankine vortex, which has constant vorticity inside the tube,
{\bm{\omega}}={\rm const}\hat{\bf z}
, and zero vorticity outside the tube,
{\bm{\omega}}=0
. The cylindrical surface of the tube experiences singularity, the jump in vorticity.

H.11
From monopole to pancake vortex sheet

Now we can adiabatically deform the inner sphere, so that the topological invariant
N=1
is conserved. Then the flow will be automatically adjusted to the new configuration of the core.

There are several interesting deformations with the conservation of axial symmetry.

One may adiabatically decrease the radius of the core. In the limit of small radius the configuration will represent the monopole with the singular core.

One may deform the inner sphere to the disk of small thickness without violating the axial symmetry and the discrete symmetry
with respect to inversion:
{\bf S}({\bf r})=-{\bf S}(-{\bf r})
,
{\bf v}({\bf r})={\bf v}(-{\bf r})
,
{\bm{\omega}}({\bf r})=-{\bm{\omega}}(-{\bf r})
. This would result in the Migdal vortex sheet.

So, let us consider the monopole structure obtained when the inner sphere in Sec.
H.10
is adiabatically (i.e. with conservation of topological invariant
N=1
) deformed to the thin disk. In the vicinity of the disk one has
S_{z}(z)=|S_{z}|\,\sign(z)
, i.e. the disk represents the domain wall, where
S_{z}
changes sign, but

|S_{z}(\rho)|
is continuous across the sheet. Velocity is
{\bf v}=ZS_{z}\nabla\phi_{2}({\bf r})
. Let us first consider

\phi_{2}({\bf r})=\phi_{2}(\phi)=\phi
, the azimuthal angle. In this case the flow is circulating in the opposite directions on two sides of the disk:

{\bf v}=ZS_{z}\nabla\phi=Z\frac{|S_{z}(\rho)|}{\rho}\sign(z){\hat{\bm{\phi}}}\,.

(H.13)

The vorticity has the
\delta(z)
singularity at the disk and
\delta_{2}({\bm{\rho}})
singularity on the axis:

{\bm{\omega}}={\hat{\bm{\rho}}}\,\delta(z)\frac{Z|S_{z}(\rho)|}{\rho}+Z\hat{\bf z}\,\sign{z}\,\left(\delta_{2}({\bm{\rho}})+\frac{1}{\rho}\partial_{\rho}|S_{z}(\rho)|\right)\,.

(H.14)

This structure represents the monopole with
N=1
and two Dirac strings, each with winding number
m=1
. The core of the monopole is represented by the disk.
On two sides of the disk one has the flow circulating in opposite directions. The singular contribution
\delta_{2}({\bm{\rho}})
to vorticity along
z
axis represents two Dirac strings with opposite circulations. These Dirac strings enter the monopole (vortex sheet disk) from two sides.

H.12
Wall bounded by string

In the present scenario, the disk itself represents the
Z_{2}
domain wall at which
S_{z}
changes sign. In the body of the disk, the vector
\hat{\bf S}_{\perp}
has
2\pi n
rotations across the disk. In the Migdal scenario the vector
\vec{S}_{\perp}
changes sign., and it makes half-integer number of rotations
(2n+1)\pi
, or, a reflection in addition to
2\pi n
rotations.
[
5
,
6
]

In both cases the edge of the disk represents the circular string (ring), which terminates the domain wall. This string has either the
\pi_{1}
winding number
n
, or the winding number
n+1/2
.
In the first case it is the analog of the soliton terminated by strings,
[
67
]
while in the second case it is the analog of Kibble-Lazarides-Shafi (KLS) wall
[
68
,
69
]
bounded by Alice strings.
The similar configuration, the domain wall bounded by Alice string (half-quantum vortex), has been experimentally studied in the polar phase of superfluid
3
He.
[
70
]
The general topology of the KLS walls bounded by string was discussed in Ref.
[
71
]
. In both cases, solitons terminated by strings and domain walls terminated by strings, the classification is in terms of the relative homotopy groups. The winding number
n
or
n+1/2
is the element of topology of such combined objects.

These combined objects are described by the combination of topologies. This includes
\pi_{2}(S^{2})=Z
(the topological charge
N=2m
of the hedhehog-monopole and the winding number of Dirac strings, which enter the monopole);

\pi_{1}(S^{1})=Z
(the winding number
n
of the soliton and of the circular ring, which terminates the soliton);
\pi_{0}(Z_{2})=Z_{2}
(the domain wall).

H.13
SO(3) hydrodynamics of incompressible liquid

Let us introduce instead of the triads with unit length
{\bf e}_{a}
, the more general triads
{\bf E}_{a}
:

{\bf E}_{1}=A{\bf e}_{1}\,,\,{\bf E}_{2}=A{\bf e}_{2}\,,\,{\bf L}={\bf E_{3}}={\bf E}_{1}\times{\bf E}_{2}=Z{\bf e}_{3}\equiv Z{\bf S}\,,

(H.15)

{\bf E}_{1}^{2}={\bf E}_{2}^{2}=A^{2}=Z\,\,,\,\,{\bf E}_{1}\cdot{\bf E}_{2}=0\,.

(H.16)

The velocity field is:

v_{i}={\bf E}_{1}\cdot\nabla_{i}{\bf E}_{2}\,.

(H.17)

Note that this is the general form of velocity, which is not violated by the condition of incompressibility. The latter condition is obtained by gauge transformation in Eq.(
H.25
).

We have the following PB for canonically conjugate variables:

\{E_{1}^{i}({\bf r}_{1}),E_{2}^{k}({\bf r}_{2})\}=\delta^{ik}\delta({\bf r}_{2}-{\bf r}_{1})\,.

(H.18)

Eq.(
H.18
) gives:

\displaystyle\{L^{i}({\bf r}_{1}),E^{k}_{a}({\bf r}_{2})\}=e^{ikl}E^{l}_{a}\delta({\bf r}_{2}-{\bf r}_{1})\,,

(H.19)

\displaystyle\{L^{i}({\bf r}_{1}),L^{k}({\bf r}_{2})\}=e^{ikl}L^{l}\delta({\bf r}_{2}-{\bf r}_{1})\,,

(H.20)

\displaystyle\{v_{i}({\bf r}_{1}),E_{k}^{a}({\bf r}_{2})\}=\nabla_{i}E_{k}^{a}\,\delta({\bf r}_{2}-{\bf r}_{1})\,\,,\,a=1,2,3\,.

(H.21)

The Eq.(
H.21
) gives the following motion equations for the tetrad fields
{\bf E}_{a}
, with
a=1,2,3
:

\dot{\bf E}_{a}+({\bf v}\cdot\nabla){\bf E}_{a}=0\,,

(H.22)

Multiplying each of 3 equations to the corresponding
{\bf E}_{a}
one obtains that each of three equation gives the equation for
Z
:

\dot{Z}+({\bf v}\cdot\nabla)Z=0\,.

(H.23)

This demonstrates that
Z
can be chosen as constant in spacetime.

The PB for velocity field are:

\{v_{i}({\bf r}_{1}),v_{k}({\bf r}_{2})\}=e_{ikl}\omega^{l}({\bf r}_{1})\delta({\bf r}_{2}-{\bf r}_{1})\,.

(H.24)

Gauge transformation which does not change the distribution of vector
{\bf L}

(and the vorticity, which is expressed in terms of
{\bf L}
), is:

{\bf E}_{1}+i{\bf E}_{2}\rightarrow({\bf E}_{1}+i{\bf E}_{2})e^{i\Phi}\,\,,\,\,{\bf v}\rightarrow{\bf v}+\nabla\Phi\,.

(H.25)

This is the rotation of the dyad
{\bf E}_{1}
and
{\bf E}_{2}
by angle
\Phi
about the local direction of
{\bf L}
-vector.
The gauge transformation is needed to satisfy the incompressibility condition
\nabla\cdot{\bf v}=0
. It is analogous to transformation from 3 Clebsch variables to 2. But as distinct from Clebsch variables, triads correctly deal with with such structures, where Clebsch variables have unphysical singularities (for example in the configuration with monopole and Dirac strings in Sec.
H.6
).

H.14
Spin connection and torsion

The metric, which follows from tetrads, is flat:

g^{ik}=\delta^{ab}E_{a}^{i}E_{b}^{k}\,.

(H.26)

Spin connection and torsion are

C^{12}_{i}=-C^{21}_{i}={\bf E}_{1}\cdot\nabla_{i}{\bf E}_{2}=-{\bf E}_{2}\cdot\nabla_{i}{\bf E}_{1}=v_{i}\ \,.

(H.27)

T^{a}_{ik}=\partial_{i}E^{a}_{k}-\partial_{k}E^{a}_{i}\ \,.

(H.28)

H.15
Conclusion

It looks that not only the relativistic quantum field theories, but also the quantum hydrodynamics of topological superfluids can be useful for study the important structures emerging in classical liquids.
